Entropy production of non-reciprocal interactions
Ziluo Zhang (张子洛), Rosalba Garcia-Millan

Abstract
Non-reciprocal interactions are present in many systems out of equilibrium. The rate of entropy production is a measure that quantifies the time irreversibility of a system, and thus how far it is from equilibrium. In this work, we introduce a non-motile active particle system where activity originates from asymmetric, pairwise interaction forces that result in an injection of energy at the microscopic scale. We calculate stationary correlation functions and entropy production rate in three exactly solvable cases, and analyse a more general case in a perturbation theory as an expansion in weak interactions using a fully microscopic description. Our results show that equilibrium may be recovered by adjusting the diffusion constants despite non-reciprocity, revealing an equivalence in the absolute amplitude of the force and diffusivity. We support our analytical results with numerical simulations.



\CJKnospace

Introduction.
—Non-reciprocal interactions are those that do not obey Newton’s
third law (
actio
equal
reactio
).
These
generate intrinsically out-of-equilibrium dynamics

Loos_2020
;
GodrecheLuck:2019
;
KanoETAL:2017

and are often invoked to
model striking dynamical pattern formation such as flocking
Cavagna_2018
;
NagyETAL:2010
,
worming
Deblais:2020
or travelling states
YouETAL:2020
.
Common mechanisms that break reciprocity are
(anti-)alignment
FruchartETAL:2021
and vision cone interactions

Durve:2018
;
Barberis:2016
;
LoosKlappMartynec:2022
.
Non-reciprocal interactions between two species play a crucial role in
numerous biological processes, such
as predator-prey dynamics
Redner_1999
;
MeredithETAL:2020

or collective cell migration
Stramer:2017
.
Non-reciprocity is found in attractive-repulsive interactions giving rise,
for example, to the chase-and-run dynamics present between a dog and
a herd of sheep
Stramer:2017
:
while the dog runs toward the sheep,
sheep fearfully run away from the dog,
following a trajectory such as in Fig.
1
(a).

Systems with non-reciprocal interactions are generally out of equilibrium,
which is characterised by the breakdown of time-reversal symmetry.
The rate of entropy production has become, over the last decade,
a prominent measure of irreversibility that quantifies
how far from equilibrium a system is

PruessnerGarcia-Millan:2022
;
Gaspard:2007
;
Cocconi:2020
;
AlstonCocconiBertrand:2022
.
Measuring and calculating the entropy production rate is, therefore, key to
understanding the non-equilibrium behaviour of a system
and poses a major theoretical challenge in the case of
interacting many-particle active systems.
Recent studies have focused on the thermodynamic properties of non-reciprocity
in a system described by coupled, linear Langevin equations

Loos_2020
;
GodrecheLuck:2019
;
BrandenbourgerETAL:2019
.
Reference
Loos_2020
showed that there exist systems with non-reciprocal interactions
that do satisfy detailed balance.
However, a fully microscopic calculation of the entropy production of a
many-particle system with non-linear and non-reciprocal interaction
forces is lacking.
The power of microscopic theories in the study of active particle systems lies in
the direct link they establish between agents’ properties and their
emergent phenomena.
Deriving exact results that serve as benchmark is thus of
paramount importance
Cocconi:2020
;
PruessnerGarcia-Millan:2022
.

Particle trajectories
(x,\mathbf{y})
described by Eq. (
1
) with
one particle of species A (red) and

N=10
particles of species B (grey),
sinusoidal interacting potentials,

The sign of potential amplitudes
a_{1}
and

are indicated on the left.
Interactions are non-reciprocal in (a) and (b) and reciprocal in (c) and (d).
In this example, since
D_{1}=D_{2}
,
non-reciprocal systems are active
and
reciprocal systems are passive.

In this letter, we introduce a non-motile
active particle system that is driven
out of equilibrium by
non-reciprocal interactions between one particle and the rest

AlstonETAL:2022
.
We use a fully microscopic description to calculate correlation
functions and entropy production.
We consider three exactly solvable cases and a fourth, more general case using
a perturbative field theory.
Our perturbative approach provides a systematic framework
to calculate correlation functions and the short-time propagator,
both of which are essential to calculate the entropy production rate

ZhangPruessner:2021
;
Garcia-MillanPruessner:2021
;
PruessnerGarcia-Millan:2022
;
BotheCocconiZhenPruessner:2022
.
Our analytical results are compared with the average rate of dissipated heat
along the stochastic trajectories of the particles in Sekimoto’s framework
Loos_2020
;
Sekimoto:2010
;
GhosalBisker:2022
;
RoldanETAL:2021
, showing good
agreement
within the domain where our perturbative treatment holds.
We address the regime of small to intermediate
particle numbers, and establish the path from microscopic dynamics
to emergent effective interactions.

Following the example of chase-and-run dynamics described above,
we consider a one-dimensional system with two types of particles:
one particle of species A at position
x

and
N
particles of species
B
at
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})
,
with diffusion constants
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
,
and drifts
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, that live on a
ring of length
L
.
The interaction forces
are mediated by the periodic and bounded pair potentials
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, resulting in the
additional effective drifts

-\sum_{i}\partial_{x}V_{1}(x-y_{i})
for particle A
and
-\partial_{y_{i}}V_{2}(y_{i}-x)
for each particle B.
According to
the structure of these pair interactions, B particles do not directly interact with
each other, although their trajectories are effectively coupled by means of particle A,
as we discuss below.

The system dynamics are described by the coupled, overdamped Langevin equations,

\displaystyle\dot{x}

\displaystyle=u_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}V^{\prime}_{1}(x-y_{i})+\xi(t)\ ,

(1a)

\displaystyle\dot{y}_{i}

\displaystyle=u_{2}-V^{\prime}_{2}(y_{i}-x)+\xi_{i}(t)\ ,

(1b)

where
\xi
and
\xi_{i}
are Gaussian white noises, with correlators

\left\langle\xi(t)\xi(t^{\prime})\right\rangle=2D_{1}\delta(t-t^{\prime})

and

\left\langle\xi_{i}(t)\xi_{j}(t^{\prime})\right\rangle=2D_{2}\delta_{i,j}\delta(t-t^{\prime})
.
We assume Boltzmann constant and mobility
to be unity,
and therefore deem
the diffusion constants to essentially
be temperatures.
The stationary probability currents of
x
and
y_{i}

are

J_{x}=-\left(-u_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}V_{1}^{\prime}(x-y_{i})+D_{1}\partial_{x}\right)P(x,\mathbf{y})

and

J_{y_{i}}=-\left(-u_{2}+V_{2}^{\prime}(y_{i}-x)+D_{2}\partial_{y_{i}}\right)P(x,\mathbf{y})
,
where
P(x,\mathbf{y})
is the joint probability density of state
x,\mathbf{y}

Risken:1989
.
If interactions are reciprocal, namely the interaction potentials satisfy

V_{1}(\ell)=V_{2}(\ell)\equiv V(\ell)
with
\ell=x-y
, then forces are conservative because
Eq. (
1
) can be derived from
the Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}=-u_{1}x+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-u_{2}y_{i}+V(x-y_{i})\right)
.
Conversely, non-reciprocal interactions result in non-conservative forces.
Fig.
1
shows particle trajectories described by
Eq. (
1
) with different combinations of attractive and repulsive
potentials, illustrating the breakdown of time-reversal symmetry
in the active systems in Figs.
1
(a) and
1
(b),
in contrast to the passive systems in Figs.
1
(c) and
1
(d).
We simulate particle trajectories described by Eq. (
1
) using standard Brownian
dynamics simulations.

We calculate
the entropy production to quantify the irreversibility of this system

Gaspard:2007
;
Cocconi:2020
;
AlstonCocconiBertrand:2022
.
In Gaspard’s framework
Gaspard:2007
, the entropy production
of a Markov process is

\displaystyle\dot{S}(t)=

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{(N!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}

\displaystyle\Bigg\{P(x,\mathbf{y};t)\dot{W}(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\tau)

\displaystyle\times\ln\left(\frac{P(x,\mathbf{y};t)W(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\tau)}{P(x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};t)W(x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\rightarrow x,\mathbf{y};\tau)}\right)\Bigg\}\ ,

(2)

where

W(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\tau)
is the transition probability for the system to go from state
x,\mathbf{y}
to state

x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
in an interval of time
\tau
.
The Gibbs factor
1/N!
in each integral over

\mathbf{y}
in Eq. (
Entropy production of non-reciprocal interactions
) accounts for the phase space of the positions of B particles being
that of
indistinguishable particles in the continuum, where the probability that two particles are found at the same position has zero measure.

Using the framework established in
PruessnerGarcia-Millan:2022
,
the entropy production in Eq. (
Entropy production of non-reciprocal interactions
) in the stationary
state
simplifies to

\displaystyle\dot{S}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}\bigg\{-V^{\prime\prime}_{1}(x-y)+\frac{1}{D_{1}}\left(\frac{u_{1}}{N}-V^{\prime}_{1}(x-y)\right)^{2}-V^{\prime\prime}_{2}(y-x)+\frac{1}{D_{2}}\left(u_{2}-V^{\prime}_{2}(y-x)\right)^{2}\bigg\}P^{(1+N)}_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}\,}\frac{1}{D_{1}}\left(\frac{u_{1}}{N}-V^{\prime}_{1}(x-y)\right)\left(\frac{u_{1}}{N}-V^{\prime}_{1}(x-y^{\prime})\right)P^{(1+N)}_{3}(x,y,y^{\prime})\ ,

(3)

which is derived in the Supplemental Material (SM).
Eq. (
3
) shows that the entropy production of this system depends
on the two-point
P^{(1+N)}_{2}
and three-point
P^{(1+N)}_{3}
correlation functions

PruessnerGarcia-Millan:2022
.
In the absence of an exact solution,
we calculate these correlation functions perturbatively,
using a Doi-Peliti field theory that captures the
microscopic dynamics of Eq. (
1
).

The cases (i) chase-and-run dynamics

, (ii) reciprocal interactions

, and

a_{1}=a_{2}D_{1}/D_{2}
, are indicated.
The system with reciprocal interactions is out of equilibrium because

D_{1}\neq D_{2}
, Eq. (
5
).

Two-particle system.
—The system with one particle of each species is tractable exactly.
Without self-propulsion,
u_{1}=u_{2}=0
, the two-point correlation function
is the barometric formula

Risken:1989
;
FehertoiPolackova:ToBePublished

P_{2}^{(2)}(x,y)=\frac{1}{L\mathcal{N}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{V_{1}(x-y)+V_{2}(y-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{V_{1}(x-y)+V_{2}(y-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{V_{1}(x-y)+V_{2}(y-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{V_{1}(x-y)+V_{2}(y-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}\ ,

(4)

with
\mathcal{N}
such that

\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}P^{(2)}_{2}(x,y)=1
, which is represented with dashed lines in Fig.
2
.
The three-point correlation function is
naturally
P_{3}^{(2)}=0
.
Setting the probability currents to zero,
J_{x}=0
and
J_{y}=0
,
gives the condition for detailed balance for this system,

\frac{V^{\prime}_{1}(x-y)}{D_{1}}=-\frac{V^{\prime}_{2}(y-x)}{D_{2}}\ ,

(5)

showing an equivalent role in the
amplitude of interaction force and diffusion.

The system
can therefore be out of equilibrium due to particle interactions
even in the absence of self-propulsion
.
The detailed balance condition in Eq. (
5
)
is consistent with the detailed balance condition found in

Loos_2020

for the special case of harmonic interaction potentials.
Using Eq. (
4
) in Eq. (
3
),
the stationary entropy production is

\dot{S}=\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}\frac{\rho(\ell)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{D_{2}}{D_{1}}}V^{\prime}_{1}(\ell)+\sqrt{\frac{D_{1}}{D_{2}}}V^{\prime}_{2}(-\ell)\right)^{2}\ ,

(6)

where
\rho(x-y)=LP_{2}^{(2)}(x,y)
.
The entropy production is zero only if Eq. (
5
) is satisfied.
Fig.
3
shows
\dot{S}
for the two-particle case,
N=1
,
for sinusoidal interaction potentials.

Equilibrium system.
—Imposing the detailed balance
condition in Eq. (
5
) and zero
drifts
u_{1}=u_{2}=0
,
the stationary joint probability density is

P(x,\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{L\mathcal{N}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{V_{1}(x-y_{i})+V_{2}(y_{i}-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{V_{1}(x-y_{i})+V_{2}(y_{i}-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{V_{1}(x-y_{i})+V_{2}(y_{i}-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{V_{1}(x-y_{i})+V_{2}(y_{i}-x)}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}\ ,

(7)

with normalisation
\mathcal{N}
such that

\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}P(x,\mathbf{y})=N!
.
The probability currents
vanish, implying that the detailed balance condition in Eq. (
5
)
generalises to the many-particle system.

Chase and run.
—The limiting case where interaction forces
between species are attractive-repulsive with equal magnitude and equal directions
is of particular interest both for its biological motivation
Stramer:2017
,
as well as for its mathematical tractability.
Assuming that interaction potentials satisfy
V_{1}(\ell)=-V_{2}(-\ell)\equiv V(\ell)
,
the two-particle system is uniformly distributed, Eq. (
4
),
because the distance
\ell=x-y
diffuses in the comoving frame, and
it is the centre of mass that spins with velocity
-V^{\prime}(\ell)
.
By the same reasoning, the stationary joint probability density stays uniform
when adding new particles to the system.
By marginalisation,
the
n
-point correlation
function is

P_{n}^{(1+N)}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})=\frac{N!}{(N-n+1)!L^{n}}\ ,

(8)

for
n\geq 1
.
The entropy production in Eq. (
3
) is, thus,

\dot{S}=\frac{N}{L}\left(\frac{1}{D_{1}}+\frac{1}{D_{2}}\right)\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}\left({V^{\prime}(\ell)}\right)^{2}\ ,

(9)

yielding an exact result for arbitrary particle number
N
.
Eq. (
9
)
shows that the system with attractive-repulsive interactions
is out of equilibrium independently of the magnitude of interaction forces,
as indicated in Fig.
3
.
Indeed, the
chaser

tends to run behind the
chased
, exhibiting the
breakdown of time-reversal symmetry.

Sinusoidal interaction potentials.
—To explore the parameter space of
attractive and repulsive interactions,
we consider sinusoidal potentials

V_{1}(\ell)=a_{1}\cos{k_{1}\ell}
and

V_{2}(\ell)=a_{2}\cos{k_{1}\ell}
,
with
k_{1}=2\pi/L
.
The signs of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
determine whether interactions are
attractive or repulsive: negative amplitudes result in attraction, whereas
positive amplitudes result in repulsion, as shown in Fig.
1
.

We derive the two-point and three-point correlation functions,
in a perturbative expansion valid at small amplitudes
a_{1}
and
a_{2}

compared to the diffusion constants
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
in the SM.
In Fig.
2
, we show the two-point correlation function

P_{2}^{(1+N)}(x,y)
of the A particle at position
x
and one out of
N
particles
of species B at
y
,
as a function of the distance
x-y
, varying
N
.
Due to attractive interactions, short distances are favoured over longer distances,
which is more prominent as
N
increases.
Fig.
2
shows good agreement for small
to intermediate
N
and
illustrates how the deviation of the
numerical estimates from the perturbative prediction increases with
N
.

Using the correlation functions
P_{2}^{(1+N)}
and
P_{3}^{(1+N)}

in Eq. (
3
) we obtain the
entropy production
\dot{S}
, derived in the SM, which is shown in
Fig.
3
as a function of
a_{1}
(solid lines).
The behaviour of
\dot{S}
is shown
for two ranges of
N
: the small to large particle number in Fig.
3
(a),
and the small to intermediate
particle numbers in Fig.
3
(b).
Following Sekimoto’s
framework
Loos_2020
;
Sekimoto:2010
;
GhosalBisker:2022
;
RoldanETAL:2021
,
in Fig.
3
we compare
the perturbative result for
\dot{S}

with the total entropy production

\dot{S}={\dot{Q}_{x}}/{\mathcal{T}_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\dot{Q}_{y_{i}}}/{\mathcal{T}_{2}}

estimated numerically from the average rate of dissipated energy
along particle trajectories,

\dot{Q}_{x}=\left\langle\left(\dot{x}(t)-\xi(t)\right)\circ\mathrm{d}x(t)\right\rangle/{\mathrm{d}t}

and

\dot{Q}_{y_{i}}=\left\langle\left(\dot{y_{i}}(t)-\xi_{i}(t)\right)\circ\mathrm{d}{y_{i}}(t)\right\rangle/{\mathrm{d}t}
,
where
\circ
denotes the Stratonovich
product and
where the temperatures are
\mathcal{T}_{1}=D_{1}/(k_{\text{B}}\mu)
,
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}=D_{2}/(k_{\text{B}}\mu)
,
with Boltzmann constant
k_{\text{B}}
and mobility
\mu

set to unity.

Fig.
3
shows that the entropy production
\dot{S}
increases smoothly for
amplitudes away from the equilibrium
a_{1}=a_{2}D_{1}/D_{2}
, and for
increasing
N
.
It further shows that
\dot{S}
grows faster in the attractive-repulsive regime
(
a_{1}<0
) than in the repulsive-repulsive regime (
a_{1}>0
).
The cases of chase-and-run dynamics, reciprocal interactions and equilibrium
are indicated. Here, since the diffusion constants are different,
D_{1}\neq D_{2}
,
the system with reciprocal interactions does not satisfy the condition for
detailed balance in Eq. (
5
) and is thus out of equilibrium.
The agreement is excellent between numerical results and
perturbative predictions within
the validity regime, that is for
|a_{1}|
and
N
sufficiently small.
Increasing
either of those parameters
leads to larger deviation of the numerical estimates
from the perturbative prediction.

Effective interactions.
—Although particles of species B do not directly interact
with each other according to
Eq. (
1
), their trajectories are correlated
through the particle of species A,
as illustrated in Fig.
1
. In Figs.
1
(c) and (d),
for instance, B particles visibly display effective attraction.
Marginalising the three-point correlation function,

\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}P^{(1+N)}_{3}(x,y,y^{\prime})=P_{B,B}^{(1+N)}(y,y^{\prime})
,
reveals correlations between any
two particles of species B at positions
y
and
y^{\prime}
,
that we identify as effective interactions, with a sign (attractive or repulsive) that depends
on the amplitudes
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, derived in the SM.
We found that B particles display effective repulsion if

a_{1}>-a_{2}>0
or
-a_{1}>a_{2}>0

(blue area in the phase diagram in Fig.
4
(a)),
no correlation if
a_{1}=-a_{2}
or
a_{2}=0
(white lines),
and effective attraction otherwise
(red area).
Based on this prediction, which is of second order in the perturbation
expansion, we explored the two-point correlation function
P_{B,B}^{(1+N)}

for an example of effective repulsion in the regime
-a_{1}>a_{2}>0
,
see Fig.
4
(b),
which shows that long distances are more frequent than short distances
between any two B particles.
The underlying microscopic mechanism is the following:
the attraction felt by particle A is stronger than the repulsion felt by B particles,
so that A captures one of the B particles, while the rest escape.

Conclusions.
—
We have studied the role of non-reciprocal interactions in the environmental
entropy change using a fully microscopic description.
We have shown that non-reciprocal forces generally break detailed balance,
resulting in a local injection of energy and turning a system of
non-motile particles into an active one.
To our knowledge, this is the first microscopic field theory of an interacting,
many-particle active system.
Our framework can be extended to estimate the extractable work in other
physical systems, such as asymmetrically shaped objects immersed in
active baths that have been proposed in the context of autonomous engines.

We are grateful to G. Pruessner for introducing us to field theory and for seminal
discussions about the barometric formula.
We also thank S. Loos, C. Scalliet, M. Cates,
T. Agranov, P. Pietzonka,
G. Biroli, B. Walter, L. Cocconi and Z. Zhen for useful discussions.
RG-M was supported in part by the European Research Council under the EU’s Horizon 2020 Programme (Grant number 740269), and acknowledges support from a St John’s College Research Fellowship, University of Cambridge.

]
,

\dot{S}=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{(N!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y^{\prime}\,}P(x,\mathbf{y})\dot{W}(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\tau)\ln\left(\frac{W(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\tau)}{W(x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\rightarrow x,\mathbf{y};\tau)}\right)\ ,

(S-1)

where
P(x,\mathbf{y})=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}P(x,\mathbf{y};t)
is the stationary density.
Following the approach in
[
15
]

we obtain the transition rate
\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}\dot{W}(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\tau)=\dot{W}(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};0)
from the Fokker-Planck Eq. (
S-7
),

\displaystyle\dot{W}(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};0)=\Bigg\{D_{1}\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{2}-u_{1}\partial_{x^{\prime}}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(D_{2}\partial_{y_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}-u_{2}\partial_{y_{i}^{\prime}}+V_{1}^{\prime}(x-y_{i})\partial_{x^{\prime}}+V_{2}^{\prime}(y_{i}-x)\partial_{y_{i}^{\prime}}\right)\Bigg\}\delta(x^{\prime}-x)\delta(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y})\ ,

(S-2)

and the short-time limit of the logarithm of the ratio of
propagators,

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0}\ln\left(\frac{W(x,\mathbf{y}\rightarrow x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\tau)}{W(x^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\rightarrow x,\mathbf{y};\tau)}\right)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\frac{x^{\prime}-x}{2D_{1}}\left(\frac{u_{1}}{N}-V^{\prime}_{1}(x-y_{i})+\frac{u_{1}}{N}-V^{\prime}_{1}(x^{\prime}-y_{i}^{\prime})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{y_{i}^{\prime}-y_{i}}{2D_{2}}\left(u_{2}-V^{\prime}_{2}(y_{i}-x)+u_{2}-V^{\prime}_{2}(y_{i}^{\prime}-x^{\prime})\right)\right\}\ .

(S-3)

Substituting Eqs. (
S-2
)
and (
S-3
) into (
S-1
),
using the normalisation that accounts for B particles being
indistinguishable

\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}P(x,\mathbf{y})=N!
, and
integrating by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{S}=\frac{1}{N!}\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{N}y\,}\bigg\{

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}V^{\prime\prime}_{1}(x-y_{i})+\frac{1}{D_{1}}\left(u_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}V^{\prime}_{1}(x-y_{i})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}V^{\prime\prime}_{2}(y_{i}-x)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{D_{2}}\left(u_{2}-V^{\prime}_{2}(y_{i}-x)\right)^{2}\bigg\}P(x,\mathbf{y})\ .

(S-4)

Using the following marginalisation property of the
n
-point correlation
function,

\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y_{n-1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y_{n-1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y_{n-1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y_{n-1}\,}P^{(1+N)}_{n}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})=(N-n+2)P^{(1+N)}_{n-1}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-2})\ ,

(S-5)

we arrive at Eq. (
3
).

In the many-particle system with sinusoidal interaction potentials and zero drift,
the leading order
behaviour of the entropy production is obtained by
substituting Eqs. (
S-28
) and (
S-32
) into Eq. (
3
),

\displaystyle\dot{S}=\frac{2\pi^{2}N}{L^{2}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{a_{1}^{2}}{D_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{D_{2}}-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{2})^{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\bigg)\bigg(1-\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)^{2}\bigg)-(N-1)\frac{a_{1}(a_{1}+a_{2})(D_{1}a_{2}-D_{2}a_{1})^{2}}{D_{1}D_{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})^{2}(4D_{1}+2D_{2})}\bigg]+\text{h.o.t.}\ ,

(S-6)

shown in Fig.
3
.
In the validity regime, the leading
order of the entropy
production in (
S-6
) is
nonnegative, as can be seen by
using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
{a_{1}^{2}}/{D_{1}}+{a_{2}^{2}}/{D_{2}}\geq{(a_{1}+a_{2})^{2}}/{(D_{1}+D_{2})}
.
In fact, the first term in the right-hand side of Eq. (
S-6
) vanishes if

a_{1}/D_{1}=a_{2}/D_{2}
, which is, precisely,
the detailed balance condition for this case, Eq. (
5
).
However, outside of this validity regime,
Eq. (
S-6
) clearly provides an
unphysical result, as the entropy production rate
is predicted to be negative.
The remedy to this unphysical result lies
in the higher order corrections in the perturbation expansion.

II
Doi-Peliti field theory of an interacting particle system

The Fokker-Planck equation of the joint probability density
P(x,\mathbf{y};t)

of the system
described by Eq. (
1
) is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}P(x,\mathbf{y};t)=

\displaystyle\bigg\{D_{1}\partial^{2}_{x}-u_{1}\partial_{x}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(D_{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{i}}-u_{2}\partial_{y_{i}}\right)\bigg\}P(x,\mathbf{y};t)

(S-7a)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigg\{\partial_{x}\left(V_{1}^{\prime}(x-y_{i})P(x,\mathbf{y};t)\right)+\partial_{y_{i}}\left(V_{2}^{\prime}(y_{i}-x)P(x,\mathbf{y};t)\right)\bigg\}\ ,

(S-7b)

where
P(x,\mathbf{y};t)
is invariant under permutations of the positions
y_{i}
due to the
indistinguishability of B particles.

The Doi-Peliti action of this stochastic process
follows from the Fokker-Planck Eq. (
S-7
)

[
29
,
30
,
15
]
.
We introduce the annihilation field
\phi(x,t)
and the
Doi-shifted creation field
\widetilde{\phi}(x,t)

for species
A and, similarly,
\psi(y,t)
and
\widetilde{\psi}(y,t)

for species B. Even though there may be multiple
particles of each species, the action functional
\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]

is derived from
the single particle dynamics
[
15
]
.
In this paper, we use the convention that an observable

\bullet
is calculated via the path integral

\left\langle\bullet\right\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\bullet\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\right)}
.
We split the action into two parts,

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}+\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, derived from
(
S-7a
), governs
free motion of particles,

\mathcal{A}_{0}=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\widetilde{\phi}\bigg\{-\partial_{t}+D_{1}\partial_{x}^{2}-u_{1}\partial_{x}\bigg\}\phi+\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}\widetilde{\psi}\bigg\{-\partial_{t}+D_{2}\partial_{y}^{2}-u_{2}\partial_{y}\bigg\}\psi\ ,

(S-8)

and
\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}
, derived from (
S-7b
),
governs particle interactions

[
31
]

\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}=-\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}\left\{(\partial_{x}\widetilde{\phi})\phi\big[\partial_{x}V_{1}(x-y)\big](\widetilde{\psi}+1)\psi+(\partial_{y}\widetilde{\psi})\psi\big[\partial_{y}V_{2}(y-x)\big](\widetilde{\phi}+1)\phi\right\}\ .

(S-9)

To derive
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, we have used that the number of particles is conserved and, therefore,

\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\widetilde{\phi}(x,t)\phi(x,t)=1

and
\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}\widetilde{\psi}(y,t)\psi(y,t)=1
.
To derive the first term in
\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}
we have used that the effect of interactions
on A particles at
x
due to the presence of B particles
at
y
is proportional to the number of B particles
y
, which is measured by the number operator
\psi^{\dagger}\psi=(\widetilde{\psi}+1)\psi
, where
\psi^{\dagger}=(\widetilde{\psi}+1)
is the creator field.
The second term in
\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}
is derived similarly.
To calculate an observable
\bullet
, we do so order
by order, in the perturbation expansion

\left\langle\bullet\right\rangle=\left\langle\bullet\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}\right)}\right\rangle_{0}=\left\langle\bullet\left({1+\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}}+\ldots\right)\right\rangle_{0}

about the Gaussian model given by
\mathcal{A}_{0}
assuming weak interactions.

We express the fields in Fourier space following the convention

\mathord{\mathop{\phi}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}(x,t)=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t+\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t+\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t+\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t+\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x\right)}\mathord{\mathop{\phi}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}_{n}(\omega)\ \text{ and }\ \mathord{\mathop{\phi}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}_{n}(\omega)=\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\omega t-\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t-\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t-\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t-\mathring{\imath}k_{n}x\right)}\mathord{\mathop{\phi}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}(x,t)\ ,

(S-10)

where
\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22=\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}{\!\mathrm{d}/\,}(2\pi)
and
k_{n}=2\pi n/L
, and
similarly for
\mathord{\mathop{\psi}\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle(\sim)}}
, as well as the interaction
potentials,

\displaystyle V_{1}(x-y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}\mu_{n}\ ,

(S-11a)

\displaystyle V_{2}(x-y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}\nu_{n}\ .

(S-11b)

The Gaussian part of the action in Eq. (
S-8
) in Fourier
space reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}

\displaystyle\left[\phi_{n}(\omega),\tilde{\phi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega^{\prime}),\psi_{n}(\omega),\tilde{\psi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega^{\prime})\right]=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{n,n^{\prime}=-\infty}^{\infty}L\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\times\left\{\tilde{\phi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega^{\prime})(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{1}k_{n}^{2}-\mathring{\imath}u_{1}k_{n})\phi_{n}(\omega)+\tilde{\psi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega^{\prime})(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{2}k_{n}^{2}-\mathring{\imath}u_{2}k_{n})\psi_{n}(\omega)\right\}\ ,

(S-12)

and the part governing interactions in
Eq. (
S-9
) is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}

\displaystyle\left[\phi_{n}(\omega_{1}),\tilde{\phi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega_{1}^{\prime}),\psi_{n}(\omega_{2}),\tilde{\psi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega_{2}^{\prime})\right]

(S-13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22({\omega_{1}}+{\omega_{1}^{\prime}}+{\omega_{2}}+{\omega_{2}^{\prime}})\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{n,n^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}}L\delta_{n+n^{\prime}+m+m^{\prime},0}

\displaystyle\times\left(\mu_{-n-n^{\prime}}k_{n^{\prime}}k_{-n-n^{\prime}}+\nu_{-m-m^{\prime}}k_{m^{\prime}}k_{-m-m^{\prime}}\right)\tilde{\phi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega_{1}^{\prime})\phi_{n}(\omega_{1})\tilde{\psi}_{m^{\prime}}(\omega_{2}^{\prime})\psi_{m}(\omega_{2})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22({\omega_{1}}+{\omega_{1}^{\prime}}+{\omega_{2}})\frac{1}{L^{3}}\sum_{n,n^{\prime},m}L\delta_{n+n^{\prime}+m,0}\mu_{-n-n^{\prime}}k_{n^{\prime}}k_{-n-n^{\prime}}\tilde{\phi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega_{1}^{\prime})\phi_{n}(\omega_{1})\psi_{m}(\omega_{2})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{1}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega_{2}^{\prime}\,}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22({\omega_{1}}+{\omega_{2}}+{\omega_{2}^{\prime}})\frac{1}{L^{3}}\sum_{n,m,m^{\prime}}L\delta_{n+m+m^{\prime},0}\nu_{-m-m^{\prime}}k_{m^{\prime}}k_{-m-m^{\prime}}\phi_{n}(\omega_{1})\tilde{\psi}_{m^{\prime}}(\omega_{2}^{\prime})\psi_{m}(\omega_{2})\ .

(S-14)

The bare propagators in Fourier space read, from Eq. (
S-12
),

\displaystyle\hbox to57.44pt{\vbox to17.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.96686pt\lower-0.59999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{n}(\omega)\widetilde{\phi}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega^{\prime})\right\rangle_{0}=\frac{L\delta_{n+n^{\prime},0}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})}{-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{1}k_{n}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}u_{1}k_{n}+r}\ ,

(S-15a)

\displaystyle\hbox to57.44pt{\vbox to19.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.96686pt\lower-3.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

function. The mass

is to be taken to zero after inverting the Fourier transform.
In Eq. (

and

represent the bare propagators of species A
and B respectively, where time is read from right to left.

(S-16c)

The dashed line

represents an interaction between two particles mediated by a potential.
Diagrammatically, a potential has a similar
representation as a propagator, with the difference
that it is represented vertically, since the
potential has no time dependence
because its effect is
propagated
instantly.

III
Two-point correlation function

III.1
Two-particle system

The stationary correlation function

P_{2}^{(2)}(x,y)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\langle\phi(x,t)\psi(y,t)\phi^{\dagger}(x_{0},t_{0})\psi^{\dagger}(y_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle

gives the probability that particles A and B
are found at positions
x
and
y
, respectively,
in the limit
t\to\infty
.
Since the system is finite and the dynamics are conservative,
the existence of the stationary limit is guaranteed.
Doi-shifting the creation fields in the observable,

\phi^{\dagger}=1+\widetilde{\phi}

and
\psi^{\dagger}=1+\widetilde{\psi}
, produces

\left\langle\phi\psi\phi^{\dagger}\psi^{\dagger}\right\rangle=\left\langle\phi\psi\widetilde{\phi}\widetilde{\psi}\right\rangle+\left\langle\phi\psi\right\rangle+\left\langle\phi\psi\widetilde{\phi}\right\rangle+\left\langle\phi\psi\widetilde{\psi}\right\rangle
, where the last three terms are all zero
because there is no matching vertex in
\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}}
.

The stationary correlation function

\displaystyle P_{2}^{(2)}(x,y)=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left\langle\phi(x,t)\psi(y,t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},t_{0})\widetilde{\psi}(y_{0},t_{0})\right\rangle\mathrel{\hat{=}}\hbox to29.55pt{\vbox to42.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.95293pt\lower-22.0519pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}

{}{}}}{

(S-17)

is independent of the initial conditions
x_{0}
,
y_{0}

since the only contributing Fourier modes in the limit
t\to\infty

are the zeroth modes
\widetilde{\phi}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{0}

[
32
,
22
]
.
This is
reflected in the diagrams by their
amputated incoming legs.
We expand the Fourier space representation of the
two-point correlation

P_{2}^{(2)}(x,y)=\frac{1}{L^{4}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}\left\langle\phi_{n}\psi_{-n}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}\widetilde{\psi}_{0}\right\rangle\ ,

(S-18)

perturbatively in small
\mu
and

\nu
compared to the diffusion constants
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
,

\left\langle\phi_{n}\psi_{-n}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}\widetilde{\psi}_{0}\right\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\left\langle\phi_{n}\psi_{-n}\widetilde{\phi}_{0}\widetilde{\psi}_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{\text{\tiny pert}})^{j}\right\rangle_{0}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}G_{j}(n)\ .

j
-th order in the perturbation expansion of the
n
-th Fourier mode of
P_{2}^{(2)}

is, diagrammatically,

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ ,

(S-20)

where the incoming legs carry the zeroth Fourier mode only.
The
j
-th order correction
G_{j}

is made of
j
four-legged interaction vertices
attached to
j
propagators of each species
forming a chain of
j-1
loops.
The
(j+1)
-th order
G_{j+1}
is generated from
G_{j}
by attaching
an interaction vertex (
S-16a
) to the outgoing legs,
effectively creating a new loop,

{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 11.81 L 35.43 -11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}

}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 62.99 31.5 M 62.99 31.5 C 62.47 32.53 61.26 33.23 59.53 33.23 L -8.35 33.23 C -10.08 33.23 -11.29 33.92 -11.81 34.96 C -12.33 33.92 -13.54 33.23 -15.27 33.23 L -83.15 33.23 C -84.88 33.23 -86.1 32.53 -86.61 31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-19.492pt}{27.60083pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hat{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{m}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}I_{n}\frac{\mu_{n-m}k_{-n}k_{n-m}+\nu_{-n+m}k_{n}k_{-n+m}}{(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{1}k_{n}^{2}+\mathring{\imath}u_{1}k_{n})(\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{2}k_{n}^{2}-\mathring{\imath}u_{2}k_{n})}G_{j}(m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{I_{n}}{L}\sum_{m}\frac{(\mu_{n-m}+\nu_{-n+m})k_{-n+m}}{(D_{1}+D_{2})k_{n}+\mathring{\imath}(u_{1}-u_{2})}G_{j}(m)\ ,

(S-21)

where
I_{n}=1-\delta_{n,0}
ensures that
G_{j}(0)=0
for
j>0
.
The zeroth-order term is, trivially, the constant zeroth Fourier
mode
G_{0}(n)=L^{2}\delta_{n,0}
.

The iterative scheme in Eq. (
S-21
) has the
same structure as the recurrence
relation found in the stationary probability
distribution of a drift-diffusive particle in
a periodic potential
[
28
]
.
Therefore, by mapping the two systems, the two-point correlation
function follows straight-forwardly,

P_{2}^{(2)}(x,y)=\rho(x-y)/L
, with

\rho(\ell)=\frac{1}{L\mathcal{N}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{V_{1}(\ell)+V_{2}(-\ell)-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell}{D_{1}+D_{2}}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{V_{1}(\ell)+V_{2}(-\ell)-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{V_{1}(\ell)+V_{2}(-\ell)-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\frac{V_{1}(\ell)+V_{2}(-\ell)-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}\int_{\ell}^{\ell+L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\ell^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{V_{1}(\ell^{\prime})+V_{2}(-\ell^{\prime})-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell^{\prime}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\frac{V_{1}(\ell^{\prime})+V_{2}(-\ell^{\prime})-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell^{\prime}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\frac{V_{1}(\ell^{\prime})+V_{2}(-\ell^{\prime})-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell^{\prime}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\frac{V_{1}(\ell^{\prime})+V_{2}(-\ell^{\prime})-(u_{1}-u_{2})\ell^{\prime}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\right)}\ ,

(S-22)

where the constant
\mathcal{N}
is such that
\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\ell\,}\rho(\ell)=1

[
27
, Eq. (11.37)]
, shown in Fig.
2
.
Although our argument is based on a
perturbation expansion about small couplings
\nu
and
\mu
,
the exact result in Eq. (
S-22
)
is valid for any couplings
because the map between
G_{j+1}
in
Eq. (
S-21
) and
the analogous
G_{j+1}

in
[
28
]

holds for all orders
j\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
.

III.2
Many-particle system

In the many-particle system, the two-point correlation function
P_{2}^{(1+N)}(x,y)

is the probability of finding the only particle
of species A at position
x
, and one out of
N
particles of
species B at
y
.
This observable
is represented as the following path integral and
diagrammatic perturbation expansion,

\displaystyle P_{2}^{(1+N)}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}N\left\langle\phi(x,t)\psi(y,t)\phi^{\dagger}(x_{0},t_{0})\psi^{\dagger}(y_{1},t_{0})\psi^{\dagger}(y_{2},t_{0})\ldots\psi^{\dagger}(y_{N},t_{0})\right\rangle

{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -23.62 11.81 L -23.62 -11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -39.37 11.81 L -39.37 35.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \mathrel{\hat{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{5}}\sum_{n,i}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)\right)}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}

\displaystyle\frac{(\mu_{i}k_{-i}k_{i}+\nu_{-i}k_{i}k_{-i})\mu_{-i}k_{-n}k_{-i}(\mu_{n}k_{-n-i}k_{n}+\nu_{-n}k_{n}k_{-n})}{(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{1}k_{n}^{2}+r)(-\mathring{\imath}(\omega+\omega^{\prime})+D_{1}k_{n+i}^{2}+r)(-\mathring{\imath}\omega^{\prime}+D_{1}k_{i}^{2}+r)(\mathring{\imath}\omega^{\prime}+D_{2}k_{i}^{2}+r)(\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{2}k_{n}^{2}+r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{2}}\frac{a_{1}(a_{1}+a_{2})(a_{1}D_{2}-a_{2}D_{1})}{4D_{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})^{2}(2D_{1}+D_{2})}{\cos{k_{1}(x-y)}}\ .

(S-27b)

Using Eqs. (
S-26
)
and (
S-27
) in
Eq. (
S-23
), we obtain
the two-point correlation function perturbatively up to third order,

\displaystyle P^{(1+N)}_{2}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\frac{N}{L^{2}}\bigg[1-\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\cos{k_{1}(x-y)}+\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\bigg)^{2}{\cos{k_{2}(x-y)}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{24}\bigg(\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\bigg)^{3}\left(3\cos{k_{1}(x-y)}-\cos{k_{3}(x-y)}\right)

\displaystyle+(N-1)\frac{a_{1}(a_{1}+a_{2})(a_{1}D_{2}-a_{2}D_{1})}{2D_{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})^{2}(2D_{1}+D_{2})}{\cos{k_{1}(x-y)}}\bigg]+\text{h.o.t.}\ .

(S-28)

IV
Three-point correlation function

The three-point correlation function of the position
x
of the A particle and positions
y
and
y^{\prime}
of two
B particles in the stationary state is

\displaystyle P_{3}^{(1+N)}(x,y,y^{\prime})=\lim_{t\to\infty}N(N-1)\left\langle\phi(x,t)\psi(y,t)\psi(y^{\prime},t)\phi^{\dagger}(x_{0},t_{0})\psi^{\dagger}(y_{1},t_{0})\psi^{\dagger}(y_{2},t_{0})\ldots\psi^{\dagger}(y_{N},t_{0})\right\rangle

(S-29)

We assume sinusoidal interaction potentials
V_{1}(\ell)=a_{1}\cos{k_{1}\ell}
and
V_{2}(\ell)=a_{2}\cos{k_{1}\ell}
,
and zero drifts
u_{1}=u_{2}=0
.
We distinguish two types of terms in Eq. (
S-29
):
those where a propagator

is
decoupled from the other two propagators, and those
where all three propagators are coupled through
interaction vertices. In the first case,
the corresponding diagrams can be calculated straightforwardly using the results in (
S-26
)
multiplying by
1/L
. For example,

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 31.89 C -1.73 31.89 -2.59 28.43 -4.32 28.43 C -5.58 28.43 -6.66 30.12 -7.78 31.89 C -8.91 33.66 -9.99 35.35 -11.24 35.35 C -12.49 35.35 -13.57 33.66 -14.7 31.89 C -15.83 30.12 -16.91 28.43 -18.16 28.43 C -19.41 28.43 -20.49 30.12 -21.62 31.89 C -22.75 33.66 -23.83 35.35 -25.08 35.35 C -26.33 35.35 -27.41 33.66 -28.54 31.89 C -29.67 30.12 -30.75 28.43 -32 28.43 C -33.25 28.43 -34.33 30.12 -35.46 31.89 C -36.59 33.66 -37.66 35.35 -38.92 35.35 L -46.06 31.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-37.32314pt}{28.92384pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -10.63 10.63 L -10.63 -10.63}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -21.26 10.63 L -21.26 31.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \mathrel{\hat{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{5}}\sum_{n,m}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)+\mathring{\imath}k_{m}(x-y^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)+\mathring{\imath}k_{m}(x-y^{\prime})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)+\mathring{\imath}k_{m}(x-y^{\prime})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{n}(x-y)+\mathring{\imath}k_{m}(x-y^{\prime})\right)}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}

\displaystyle\frac{(\mu_{m}k_{-n-m}k_{m}+\nu_{-m}k_{m}k_{-m})(\mu_{n}k_{-n}k_{n}+\nu_{-n}k_{n}k_{-n})}{(-\mathring{\imath}(\omega+\omega^{\prime})+D_{1}k_{n+m}^{2}+r)(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{1}k_{n}^{2}+r)(\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{2}k_{n}^{2}+r)(\mathring{\imath}\omega^{\prime}+D_{2}k_{m}^{2}+r)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2L^{3}}\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\bigg\{\frac{a_{2}}{2D_{2}}\cos{k_{1}(y-y^{\prime})}+\frac{a_{2}+2a_{1}}{4D_{1}+2D_{2}}\cos{k_{1}(2x-y-y^{\prime})}\bigg\}\ .

(S-31)

In fact, Eq. (
S-31
) is invariant under exchange of

y
and
y^{\prime}
, so the two last terms in the right-hand side of (
S-29
) are therefore equal.
We obtain the
three-point correlation function up to second order in
the perturbation expansion
by using Eqs. (
S-26
)
and (
S-31
),
in Eq. (
S-29
),

\displaystyle P^{(1+N)}_{3}(x,y,y^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\frac{N(N-1)}{L^{3}}\bigg[1-\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}(\cos{k_{1}(x-y)}+\cos{k_{1}(x-y^{\prime})})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{D_{1}+D_{2}}\bigg)^{2}({\cos{k_{2}(x-y)}}+{\cos{k_{2}(x-y^{\prime})}})

\displaystyle+\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}}{2(D_{1}+D_{2})}\bigg\{\frac{a_{2}}{D_{2}}\cos{k_{1}(y-y^{\prime})}+\frac{2a_{1}+a_{2}}{2D_{1}+D_{2}}\cos{k_{1}(2x-y-y^{\prime})}\bigg\}\bigg]+\text{h.o.t.}\ .

(S-32)

The
correlation functions in
Eqs. (
S-28
) and
(
S-32
) can also be
obtained by performing a Taylor expansion
about the equilibrium distribution in Eq. (
7
),
with
a_{1}/D_{1}=a_{2}/D_{2}
, where the normalisation
constant also needs to be expanded.

Marginalising Eq. (
S-32
), we obtain the
two-point correlation function between
two particles of species B at positions
y
and
y^{\prime}
,

P_{B,B}^{(1+N)}(y,y^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{L}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}P^{(1+N)}_{3}(x,y,y^{\prime})=\frac{N(N-1)}{L^{2}}\left[1+\frac{a_{2}(a_{1}+a_{2})}{2D_{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})}\cos k_{1}(y-y^{\prime})\right]+\text{h.o.t.}\ ,

(S-33)

whose second-order correction indicates the effective interactions between particles of species B,
shown in Fig.
4
.
